Kind of empty, I guess. Sometimes I can get distracted from it for a bit, but it doesn’t really feel like it goes away — it’s just quieter for a while and then I notice it again.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there have been any periods of time in the last few months where you felt significantly better for a few days or more, where the low mood was noticeably less intense or absent?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are times where it’s less noticeable, like if I’m busy with work or talking to someone and I’m distracted for a bit, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of days where I felt clearly better or like the heaviness was gone.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It feels like for a long time now I’ve just been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling in the background. Even when work is going okay, or people are nice to me, or there isn’t some obvious problem I can point to, I still feel down and kind of empty most days.\n\nSo maybe it changes in intensity a little, but I can’t really remember a few days in a row where I thought, “Oh, I’m back to normal,” or where the low mood was really absent. It’s more like it’s always there, just sometimes quieter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer care about or look forward to activities you used to enjoy, even when you’re not feeling tired or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it cleanly like that, honestly. Like, when I think about things I used to enjoy, it’s hard for me to tell whether I don’t look forward to them because I truly don’t care anymore, or because by the time they come up I already feel so worn down that looking forward to anything feels kind of muted.\n\nThere are definitely times where something sounds okay in theory, but I don’t really get that excited feeling beforehand. But I don’t know if I’d say I “no longer care” about it. That feels too strong, maybe. It’s more like my reaction is dulled. Like I know I should want to do it, or I remember that I used to enjoy it, but the anticipation isn’t really there in the same way.\n\nIf I’m not tired or overwhelmed… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t paid close enough attention to those moments, because most of the time it all kind of blends together. I wouldn’t say there’s some clear thing where I’m rested and calm and still feel absolutely nothing. It’s more vague than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what your mood is like first thing in the morning, before the day gets going — do you already feel that heaviness, or does it tend to come on later?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s usually there pretty early. Like, I don’t wake up and feel neutral and then the day slowly wears me down. A lot of mornings, before anything has even happened, there’s already this sort of flat heaviness there.\n\nSometimes I’ll lie there for a minute and kind of check in with myself, like, “Okay, what am I feeling?” and it’s just… low. Not always dramatic or intense, but it’s there. Even before work emails, before talking to anyone, before there’s some specific reason to feel bad. It’s more like I’m starting the day already carrying something.\n\nAnd then the day can change the volume of it, I guess. If work is going okay or someone is nice to me, I can get pulled out of it a little, or distracted. But underneath that, I still feel kind of down and empty most days. That’s part of why I came in — it’s been going on long enough that it doesn’t feel like it’s just reacting to one bad morning or one stressful day.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty, maybe thinking that you’re a burden or that you’ve failed in some way, even when there isn’t a clear reason for those feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get that. Maybe not in a big dramatic way all the time, but it’s kind of a constant undercurrent.\n\nAt work, for example, people still bring up this dashboard I built, like it was really useful or impressive or whatever, and instead of feeling proud, my first reaction is almost like, “They don’t realize I just got lucky,” or “I must have somehow made them think I’m better at this than I actually am.” Like I fooled them without meaning to.\n\nAnd then if I miss a follow-up, or I’m late replying to an email, even if it’s not a huge deal, my brain grabs onto that immediately as proof. Like, “See? This is what you’re actually like. You’re not as capable as they think. You’re going to let people down.” It feels like one small mistake cancels out anything I did well.\n\nI don’t always have a clear reason for feeling that way. Objectively, I know people aren’t sitting there keeping score the way I am, but it still feels like I’m failing somehow, or like I’m barely holding up this image that other people have of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’ve lost interest in things you used to care about, even when you’re not feeling particularly tired or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d call it a clean “lost interest,” if that makes sense. I know that might sound like I’m dodging the question, but it’s genuinely hard for me to separate.\n\nLike, there are things I used to care about, and I can still recognize that I care about them on some level. It’s not like I suddenly hate them or think they’re pointless. But the feeling around them is… weaker, maybe. More distant. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do that,” or “That used to make me feel good,” but I don’t get much of a pull toward it.\n\nThe part about “even when I’m not tired or overwhelmed” is the part I have trouble answering, because I feel like by the time I notice I’m not interested, I’m usually already kind of drained or mentally checked out. So I don’t know if the interest is gone on its own, or if it’s just buried under everything else.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I don’t look forward to things the way I used to, but I can’t confidently say it happens totally separately from feeling worn down. It’s more like everything feels muted, and I’m not always sure what’s causing what.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt rested and not overwhelmed — maybe after a day off or a good night’s sleep — and during that time, did you feel any renewed interest or enjoyment in activities you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example, and I’m not sure I have one. Like, I can picture days where I technically got enough sleep or had a day off, but I don’t know that I would describe myself as really rested in a way that makes the answer obvious.\n\nThere are weekends where I’ll sleep in and there isn’t anything urgent happening, and I’ll think, “Okay, maybe today I’ll actually want to do something,” but then when I’m looking at the things I used to enjoy, I still don’t always feel that spark. Or I can’t tell if I’m not interested because the interest isn’t there, or because I’m already anticipating that it’ll take effort and maybe not feel rewarding enough.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it and say, “No, never, I feel nothing,” because that sounds too absolute. But I also can’t remember a recent time where I woke up rested and suddenly felt this clear renewed enjoyment, like, “Oh yeah, I’m back, I want to do all these things again.” It’s more vague than that. Sometimes I can do something and it’s fine once I’m in it, but the wanting beforehand… that part still feels kind of muted, even when there isn’t a specific crisis or pressure that day.\n\nDoctor: So you mentioned that your mood is usually low most of the time — can you confirm if there have been any days at all in the past several weeks where you felt clearly better, even if just for a few hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, there are moments where I can seem a little better, I guess. Like if something at work goes smoothly, or someone is nice to me, or I’m distracted for a bit, I might not be actively thinking about how bad I feel. So in that sense, yes, there are a few hours here and there where it’s not as noticeable.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I feel clearly better in the way I think you mean. It’s more like the heaviness gets pushed to the background for a while. I came in because for a long time now I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling, like even when work is going okay or people are treating me well, I still feel down and kind of empty most days.\n\nSo if you ask whether there are brief windows where I can function or look normal, yes. But if you mean do I have days where I feel like myself again, or genuinely lighter, that’s much harder to say. Mostly it’s still there, just quieter sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there have been any stretches of time in the past few weeks — even just two or three days in a row — where you felt noticeably better, where the low mood seemed to ease up for a while, even if it came back afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I’ve been trying to think about that because I know a few hours here and there can blur together, but I can’t remember a solid stretch of two or three days where I thought, “Okay, this has actually lifted.”\n\nIt’s more that the low feeling is just kind of… the baseline. Like I came in because this has been going on for a long time, where I’m walking around with this flat, heavy feeling most days. Even when work is fine, even when nothing bad is happening, even when people are nice to me, I still feel down and kind of empty underneath it.\n\nThere might be parts of a day where I’m distracted or I’m functioning better, but I don’t think I’ve had a run of days recently where I felt noticeably better in a real way. If it eases up, it’s more like it gets quieter for a bit, not like it actually goes away.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy, and whether you still feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I have a really clear example, honestly. I can think of things I used to do more easily, like watching certain shows, playing a game, listening to music, stuff like that, but when I try to answer whether I still get pleasure from them now, it’s kind of hard to pin down.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll put something on because I remember that I used to enjoy it, or because it feels like the kind of thing I should do to relax, but I don’t know if I’m actually enjoying it in the same way. A lot of the time it’s more like I’m passing time. I might be occupied, and maybe there are moments where I think, “Okay, this is fine,” but it doesn’t really stay with me as satisfying afterward.\n\nI don’t want to say I get absolutely nothing from anything, because that seems too extreme. But if you’re asking about that real feeling of looking forward to it, getting into it, feeling better because I did it… that’s the part that feels pretty muted. Sometimes I can do the activity, but the pleasure part is not really obvious to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’ve been trying to think about that because I know a few hours here and there can blur together, but I can’t remember a solid stretch of two or three days where I thought, “Okay, this has actually lifted.”\n\nIt’s more that the low feeling is just kind of… the baseline. Like I came in because this has been going on for a long time, where I’m walking around with this flat, heavy feeling most days. Even when work is fine, even when nothing bad is happening, even when people are nice to me, I still feel down and kind of empty underneath it.\n\nThere might be parts of a day where I’m distracted or I’m functioning better, but I don’t think I’ve had a run of days recently where I felt noticeably better in a real way. If it eases up, it’s more like it gets quieter for a bit, not like it actually goes away.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy, and whether you still feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I have a really clear example, honestly. I can think of things I used to do more easily, like watching certain shows, playing a game, listening to music, stuff like that, but when I try to answer whether I still get pleasure from them now, it’s kind of hard to pin down.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll put something on because I remember that I used to enjoy it, or because it feels like the kind of thing I should do to relax, but I don’t know if I’m actually enjoying it in the same way. A lot of the time it’s more like I’m passing time. I might be occupied, and maybe there are moments where I think, “Okay, this is fine,” but it doesn’t really stay with me as satisfying afterward.\n\nI don’t want to say I get absolutely nothing from anything, because that seems too extreme. But if you’re asking about that real feeling of looking forward to it, getting into it, feeling better because I did it… that’s the part that feels pretty muted. Sometimes I can do the activity, but the pleasure part is not really obvious to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of a time recently when you did something you used to really enjoy, and during that moment, you genuinely felt pleasure or satisfaction from it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m trying to think of one, and honestly I’m not sure I can point to a recent moment where I’d say, yes, that was genuine pleasure or satisfaction.

Like, I’ve done things that used to be enjoyable. I’ve put on music, or watched something I used to like, or messed around with a game for a while. And in the moment I might not be miserable exactly — sometimes it’s more neutral, like I’m occupied and time is passing. But when I check in with myself, it doesn’t really feel like, “Oh, I’m glad I did that,” or “That felt good.” It’s more like I was trying to recreate a feeling I remember having, but it doesn’t quite show up.

There might be tiny moments where I smile at something or think something is kind of funny, but it feels very brief and thin, if that makes sense. I don’t really come away from it feeling satisfied. So… no, I can’t really think of a clear recent example where I did something I used to really enjoy and actually felt that same pleasure from it.

By the time I’m supposed to do something, I’m already exhausted, and that makes it hard to tell whether I’m not interested or just too drained to care.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your mood feels throughout the day — do you generally feel down most of the time, or does it come and go in waves?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly there, honestly. It feels like for a long time now I’ve just been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling in the background. It’s more like it’s always there, just sometimes quieter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer care about or look forward to activities you used to enjoy, even when you’re not feeling tired or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it cleanly like that, honestly. Like, when I think about things I used to enjoy, it’s hard for me to tell whether I don’t look forward to them because I truly don’t care anymore, or because by the time they come up I already feel so worn down that looking forward to anything feels kind of muted.\n\nThere are definitely times where something sounds okay in theory, but I don’t really get that excited feeling beforehand. But I don’t know if I’d say I “no longer care” about it. That feels too strong, maybe. It’s more like my reaction is dulled. Like I know I should want to do it, or I remember that I used to enjoy it, but the anticipation isn’t really there in the same way.\n\nIf I’m not tired or overwhelmed… I don’t know. Like, I don’t wake up and feel neutral and then the day slowly wears me down. A lot of mornings, before anything has even happened, there’s already this sort of flat heaviness there.\n\nSometimes I’ll lie there for a minute and kind of check in with myself, like, “Okay, what am I feeling?” and it’s just… low. It’s more like I’m starting the day already carrying something.\n\nAnd then the day can change the volume of it, I guess. If work is going okay or someone is nice to me, I can get pulled out of it a little, or distracted. But underneath that, I still feel kind of down and empty most days. That’s part of why I came in — it’s been going on long enough that it doesn’t feel like it’s just reacting to one bad morning or one stressful day.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty, maybe thinking that you’re a burden or that you’ve failed in some way, even when there isn’t a clear reason for those feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get that. Maybe not in a big dramatic way all the time, but it’s kind of a constant undercurrent.\n\nAt work, for example, people still bring up this dashboard I built, like it was really useful or impressive or whatever, and instead of feeling proud, my first reaction is almost like, “They don’t realize I just got lucky,” or “I must have somehow made them think I’m better at this than I actually am.” Like I fooled them without meaning to.\n\nAnd then if I miss a follow-up, or I’m late replying to an email, even if it’s not a huge deal, my brain grabs onto that immediately as proof. Like, “See? This is what you’re actually like. You’re not as capable as they think. You’re going to let people down.” It feels like one small mistake cancels out anything I did well.\n\nI don’t always have a clear reason for feeling that way. Objectively, I know people aren’t sitting there keeping score the way I am, but it still feels like I’m failing somehow, or like I’m barely holding up this image that other people have of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’ve lost interest in things you used to care about, even when you’re not feeling particularly tired or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d call it a clean “lost interest,” if that makes sense. I know that might sound like I’m dodging the question, but it’s genuinely hard for me to separate.\n\nLike, there are things I used to care about, and I can still recognize that I care about them on some level. It’s not like I suddenly hate them or think they’re pointless. But the feeling around them is… weaker, maybe. More distant. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do that,” or “That used to make me feel good,” but I don’t get much of a pull toward it.\n\nThe part about “even when I’m not tired or overwhelmed” is the part I have trouble answering, because I feel like by the time I notice I’m not interested, I’m usually already kind of drained or mentally checked out. So I don’t know if the interest is gone on its own, or if it’s just buried under everything else.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I don’t look forward to things the way I used to, but I can’t confidently say it happens totally separately from feeling worn down. It’s more like everything feels muted, and I’m not always sure what’s causing what.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt rested and not overwhelmed — maybe after a day off or a good night’s sleep — and during that time, did you feel any renewed interest or enjoyment in activities you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example, and I’m not sure I have one. It’s not like every single thing feels awful or pointless. I can still have moments where something is funny or distracting or nice. It just doesn’t seem to last very long, or it doesn’t really change the overall mood much.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back to the past few weeks — have you noticed a loss of interest in activities you used to enjoy, or has it felt like doing things takes more effort than before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the effort part is the bigger thing I notice. It’s not always that I think, “I don’t care about this anymore,” exactly. It’s more like by the afternoon I’m just so wiped out that even small things feel weirdly heavy.\n\nLike at work, if there’s a mistake in a spreadsheet, something I know how to fix and that shouldn’t be a big deal, it can feel like I’m trying to push my brain through mud. Or if I have expense paperwork to finish, it’s not complicated, but I’ll stare at it and it just feels like this huge thing. And then outside of work, even getting myself to soccer — which is something I normally want to do, or at least used to want to do — can feel like dragging my body there rather than just going.\n\nSo I guess it’s less like I’ve completely stopped liking everything, and more like everything takes more energy than it should. By the time I’m supposed to do something, I’m already exhausted, and that makes it hard to tell whether I’m not interested or just too drained to care.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your mood feels throughout the day — do you generally feel down most of the time, or does it come and go in waves?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly there, honestly. I mean, it can shift a little depending on what’s happening during the day, but the baseline is usually low. It’s not like I wake up feeling great and then suddenly crash for an hour and then I’m fine again. It’s more like I’m walking around with this flat, heavy feeling most days.\n\nEven if work is going okay, or someone is nice to me, or nothing is specifically wrong, I still feel down underneath it. Kind of empty, I guess. Sometimes I can get distracted from it for a bit, but it doesn’t really feel like it goes away — it’s just quieter for a while and then I notice it again.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there have been any periods of time in the last few months where you felt significantly better for a few days or more, where the low mood was noticeably less intense or absent?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are times where it’s less noticeable, like if I’m busy with work or talking to someone and I’m distracted for a bit, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of days where I felt clearly better or like the heaviness was gone.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It feels like for a long time now I’ve just been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling in the background. Even when work is going okay, or people are nice to me, or there isn’t some obvious problem I can point to, I still feel down and kind of empty most days.\n\nSo maybe it changes in intensity a little, but I can’t really remember a few days in a row where I thought, “Oh, I’m back to normal,” or where the low mood was really absent. It’s more like it’s always there, just sometimes quieter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer care about or look forward to activities you used to enjoy, even when you’re not feeling tired or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it cleanly like that, honestly. Like, when I think about things I used to enjoy, it’s hard for me to tell whether I don’t look forward to them because I truly don’t care anymore, or because by the time they come up I already feel so worn down that looking forward to anything feels kind of muted.\n\nThere are definitely times where something sounds okay in theory, but I don’t really get that excited feeling beforehand. But I don’t know if I’d say I “no longer care” about it. That feels too strong, maybe. It’s more like my reaction is dulled. Like I know I should want to do it, or I remember that I used to enjoy it, but the anticipation isn’t really there in the same way.\n\nIf I’m not tired or overwhelmed… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t paid close enough attention to those moments, because most of the time it all kind of blends together. I wouldn’t say there’s some clear thing where I’m rested and calm and still feel absolutely nothing. It’s more vague than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what your mood is like first thing in the morning, before the day gets going — do you already feel that heaviness, or does it tend to come on later?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s usually there pretty early. Like, I don’t wake up and feel neutral and then the day slowly wears me down. A lot of mornings, before anything has even happened, there’s already this sort of flat heaviness there.\n\nSometimes I’ll lie there for a minute and kind of check in with myself, like, “Okay, what am I feeling?” and it’s just… low. Not always dramatic or intense, but it’s there. Even before work emails, before talking to anyone, before there’s some specific reason to feel bad. It’s more like I’m starting the day already carrying something.\n\nAnd then the day can change the volume of it, I guess. If work is going okay or someone is nice to me, I can get pulled out of it a little, or distracted. But underneath that, I still feel kind of down and empty most days. That’s part of why I came in — it’s been going on long enough that it doesn’t feel like it’s just reacting to one bad morning or one stressful day.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty, maybe thinking that you’re a burden or that you’ve failed in some way, even when there isn’t a clear reason for those feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get that. Maybe not in a big dramatic way all the time, but it’s kind of a constant undercurrent.\n\nAt work, for example, people still bring up this dashboard I built, like it was really useful or impressive or whatever, and instead of feeling proud, my first reaction is almost like, “They don’t realize I just got lucky,” or “I must have somehow made them think I’m better at this than I actually am.” Like I fooled them without meaning to.\n\nAnd then if I miss a follow-up, or I’m late replying to an email, even if it’s not a huge deal, my brain grabs onto that immediately as proof. Like, “See? This is what you’re actually like. You’re not as capable as they think. You’re going to let people down.” It feels like one small mistake cancels out anything I did well.\n\nI don’t always have a clear reason for feeling that way. Objectively, I know people aren’t sitting there keeping score the way I am, but it still feels like I’m failing somehow, or like I’m barely holding up this image that other people have of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’ve lost interest in things you used to care about, even when you’re not feeling particularly tired or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d call it a clean “lost interest,” if that makes sense. I know that might sound like I’m dodging the question, but it’s genuinely hard for me to separate.\n\nLike, there are things I used to care about, and I can still recognize that I care about them on some level. It’s not like I suddenly hate them or think they’re pointless. But the feeling around them is… weaker, maybe. More distant. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do that,” or “That used to make me feel good,” but I don’t get much of a pull toward it.\n\nThe part about “even when I’m not tired or overwhelmed” is the part I have trouble answering, because I feel like by the time I notice I’m not interested, I’m usually already kind of drained or mentally checked out. So I don’t know if the interest is gone on its own, or if it’s just buried under everything else.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I don’t look forward to things the way I used to, but I can’t confidently say it happens totally separately from feeling worn down. It’s more like everything feels muted, and I’m not always sure what’s causing what.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt rested and not overwhelmed — maybe after a day off or a good night’s sleep — and during that time, did you feel any renewed interest or enjoyment in activities you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example, and I’m not sure I have one. Like, I can picture days where I technically got enough sleep or had a day off, but I don’t know that I would describe myself as really rested in a way that makes the answer obvious.\n\nThere are weekends where I’ll sleep in and there isn’t anything urgent happening, and I’ll think, “Okay, maybe today I’ll actually want to do something,” but then when I’m looking at the things I used to enjoy, I still don’t always feel that spark. Or I can’t tell if I’m not interested because the interest isn’t there, or because I’m already anticipating that it’ll take effort and maybe not feel rewarding enough.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it and say, “No, never, I feel nothing,” because that sounds too absolute. But I also can’t remember a recent time where I woke up rested and suddenly felt this clear renewed enjoyment, like, “Oh yeah, I’m back, I want to do all these things again.” It’s more vague than that. Sometimes I can do something and it’s fine once I’m in it, but the wanting beforehand… that part still feels kind of muted, even when there isn’t a specific crisis or pressure that day.\n\nDoctor: So you mentioned that your mood is usually low most of the time — can you confirm if there have been any days at all in the past several weeks where you felt clearly better, even if just for a few hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, there are moments where I can seem a little better, I guess. Like if something at work goes smoothly, or someone is nice to me, or I’m distracted for a bit, I might not be actively thinking about how bad I feel. So in that sense, yes, there are a few hours here and there where it’s not as noticeable.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I feel clearly better in the way I think you mean. It’s more like the heaviness gets pushed to the background for a while. I came in because for a long time now I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling, like even when work is going okay or people are treating me well, I still feel down and kind of empty most days.\n\nSo if you ask whether there are brief windows where I can function or look normal, yes. But if you mean do I have days where I feel like myself again, or genuinely lighter, that’s much harder to say. Mostly it’s still there, just quieter sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there have been any stretches of time in the past few weeks — even just two or three days in a row — where you felt noticeably better, where the low mood seemed to ease up for a while, even if it came back afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I’ve been trying to think about that because I know a few hours here and there can blur together, but I can’t remember a solid stretch of two or three days where I thought, “Okay, this has actually lifted.”\n\nIt’s more that the low feeling is just kind of… the baseline. Even when work is fine, even when nothing bad is happening, even when people are nice to me, I still feel down and kind of empty underneath it.\n\nThere might be parts of a day where I’m distracted or I’m functioning better, but I don’t think I’ve had a run of days recently where I felt noticeably better in a real way. If it eases up, it’s more like it gets quieter for a bit, not like it actually goes away.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy, and whether you still feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I have a really clear example, honestly. I can think of things I used to do more easily, like watching certain shows, playing a game, listening to music, stuff like that, but when I try to answer whether I still get pleasure from them now, it’s kind of hard to pin down.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll put something on because I remember that I used to enjoy it, or because it feels like the kind of thing I should do to relax, but I don’t know if I’m actually enjoying it in the same way. A lot of the time it’s more like I’m passing time. I might be occupied, and maybe there are moments where I think, “Okay, this is fine,” but it doesn’t really stay with me as satisfying afterward.\n\nI don’t want to say I get absolutely nothing from anything, because that seems too extreme. But if you’re asking about that real feeling of looking forward to it, getting into it, feeling better because I did it… that’s the part that feels pretty muted. Sometimes I can do the activity, but the pleasure part is not really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you did something you used to really enjoy, and during that moment, you genuinely felt pleasure or satisfaction from it?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and honestly I’m not sure I can point to a recent moment where I’d say, yes, that was genuine pleasure or satisfaction.\n\nLike, I’ve done things that used to be enjoyable. Yes… that’s pretty much how it’s been. I can’t really point to a few days in a row where I felt like it had lifted in any meaningful way.

I mean, there are moments where I’m distracted or I’m doing what I need to do, so maybe from the outside it doesn’t look constant. Not like one bad day or a rough week, but this kind of flat, heavy feeling that’s just there most days. It’s hard to explain because on paper things can be… fine, I guess. Work might be going okay, someone might say something nice to me, nothing obvious is wrong in that exact moment, but I still feel low. Kind of empty, like I’m moving through everything without really feeling much of a lift from it.\n\nAnd I think I’ve gotten used to carrying it around, which is part of why I didn’t come in sooner. But lately I’ve been realizing that it’s not really going away, and I’m tired of feeling like this all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that activities feel more like a chore than something you look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s exactly that. Like, I still do some of the things I normally do, and sometimes they’re okay once I’m actually doing them. It’s more that I don’t really look forward to much in the same clear way, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure if that’s because I don’t enjoy things, or because I’m just kind of tired of everything feeling the same.\n\nI guess I haven’t been paying close attention to whether something is “pleasurable” or not. I mostly just notice that I go through the motions. If I make plans, I’ll usually follow through, but there isn’t always that feeling of being excited beforehand. And then afterward I’m not always like, “Wow, I’m glad I did that.” It’s more like, okay, that happened.\n\nBut I don’t want to overstate it either. It’s not like every single thing feels awful or pointless. I can still have moments where something is funny or distracting or nice. It just doesn’t seem to last very long, or it doesn’t really change the overall mood much.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back to the past few weeks — have you noticed a loss of interest in activities you used to enjoy, or has it felt like doing things takes more effort than before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the effort part is the bigger thing I notice. It’s not always that I think, “I don’t care about this anymore,” exactly. It’s more like by the afternoon I’m just so wiped out that even small things feel weirdly heavy.\n\nLike at work, if there’s a mistake in a spreadsheet, something I know how to fix and that shouldn’t be a big deal, it can feel like I’m trying to push my brain through mud. Or if I have expense paperwork to finish, it’s not complicated, but I’ll stare at it and it just feels like this huge thing. And then outside of work, even getting myself to soccer — which is something I normally want to do, or at least used to want to do — can feel like dragging my body there rather than just going.\n\nSo I guess it’s less like I’ve completely stopped liking everything, and more like everything takes more energy than it should. By the time I’m supposed to do something, I’m already exhausted, and that makes it hard to tell whether I’m not interested or just too drained to care.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your mood feels throughout the day — do you generally feel down most of the time, or does it come and go in waves?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly there, honestly. I mean, it can shift a little depending on what’s happening during the day, but the baseline is usually low. It’s not like I wake up feeling great and then suddenly crash for an hour and then I’m fine again. It’s more like I’m walking around with this flat, heavy feeling most days.\n\nEven if work is going okay, or someone is nice to me, or nothing is specifically wrong, I still feel down underneath it. Kind of empty, I guess. Sometimes I can get distracted from it for a bit, but it doesn’t really feel like it goes away — it’s just quieter for a while and then I notice it again.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there have been any periods of time in the last few months where you felt significantly better for a few days or more, where the low mood was noticeably less intense or absent?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are times where it’s less noticeable, like if I’m busy with work or talking to someone and I’m distracted for a bit, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of days where I felt clearly better or like the heaviness was gone.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It feels like for a long time now I’ve just been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling in the background. Even when work is going okay, or people are nice to me, or there isn’t some obvious problem I can point to, I still feel down and kind of empty most days.\n\nSo maybe it changes in intensity a little, but I can’t really remember a few days in a row where I thought, “Oh, I’m back to normal,” or where the low mood was really absent. It’s more like it’s always there, just sometimes quieter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer care about or look forward to activities you used to enjoy, even when you’re not feeling tired or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it cleanly like that, honestly. Like, when I think about things I used to enjoy, it’s hard for me to tell whether I don’t look forward to them because I truly don’t care anymore, or because by the time they come up I already feel so worn down that looking forward to anything feels kind of muted.\n\nThere are definitely times where something sounds okay in theory, but I don’t really get that excited feeling beforehand. But I don’t know if I’d say I “no longer care” about it. That feels too strong, maybe. It’s more like my reaction is dulled. Like I know I should want to do it, or I remember that I used to enjoy it, but the anticipation isn’t really there in the same way.\n\nIf I’m not tired or overwhelmed… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t paid close enough attention to those moments, because most of the time it all kind of blends together. I wouldn’t say there’s some clear thing where I’m rested and calm and still feel absolutely nothing. It’s more vague than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what your mood is like first thing in the morning, before the day gets going — do you already feel that heaviness, or does it tend to come on later?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s usually there pretty early. Like, I don’t wake up and feel neutral and then the day slowly wears me down. A lot of mornings, before anything has even happened, there’s already this sort of flat heaviness there.\n\nSometimes I’ll lie there for a minute and kind of check in with myself, like, “Okay, what am I feeling?” and it’s just… low. Not always dramatic or intense, but it’s there. Even before work emails, before talking to anyone, before there’s some specific reason to feel bad. It’s more like I’m starting the day already carrying something.\n\nAnd then the day can change the volume of it, I guess. If work is going okay or someone is nice to me, I can get pulled out of it a little, or distracted. But underneath that, I still feel kind of down and empty most days. That’s part of why I came in — it’s been going on long enough that it doesn’t feel like it’s just reacting to one bad morning or one stressful day.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty, maybe thinking that you’re a burden or that you’ve failed in some way, even when there isn’t a clear reason for those feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get that. Maybe not in a big dramatic way all the time, but it’s kind of a constant undercurrent.\n\nAt work, for example, people still bring up this dashboard I built, like it was really useful or impressive or whatever, and instead of feeling proud, my first reaction is almost like, “They don’t realize I just got lucky,” or “I must have somehow made them think I’m better at this than I actually am.” Like I fooled them without meaning to.\n\nAnd then if I miss a follow-up, or I’m late replying to an email, even if it’s not a huge deal, my brain grabs onto that immediately as proof. Like, “See? This is what you’re actually like. You’re not as capable as they think. You’re going to let people down.” It feels like one small mistake cancels out anything I did well.\n\nI don’t always have a clear reason for feeling that way. Objectively, I know people aren’t sitting there keeping score the way I am, but it still feels like I’m failing somehow, or like I’m barely holding up this image that other people have of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’ve lost interest in things you used to care about, even when you’re not feeling particularly tired or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d call it a clean “lost interest,” if that makes sense. I know that might sound like I’m dodging the question, but it’s genuinely hard for me to separate.\n\nLike, there are things I used to care about, and I can still recognize that I care about them on some level. It’s not like I suddenly hate them or think they’re pointless. But the feeling around them is… weaker, maybe. More distant. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do that,” or “That used to make me feel good,” but I don’t get much of a pull toward it.\n\nThe part about “even when I’m not tired or overwhelmed” is the part I have trouble answering, because I feel like by the time I notice I’m not interested, I’m usually already kind of drained or mentally checked out. So I don’t know if the interest is gone on its own, or if it’s just buried under everything else.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I don’t look forward to things the way I used to, but I can’t confidently say it happens totally separately from feeling worn down. It’s more like everything feels muted, and I’m not always sure what’s causing what.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt rested and not overwhelmed — maybe after a day off or a good night’s sleep — and during that time, did you feel any renewed interest or enjoyment in activities you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example, and I’m not sure I have one. Like, I can picture days where I technically got enough sleep or had a day off, but I don’t know that I would describe myself as really rested in a way that makes the answer obvious.\n\nThere are weekends where I’ll sleep in and there isn’t anything urgent happening, and I’ll think, “Okay, maybe today I’ll actually want to do something,” but then when I’m looking at the things I used to enjoy, I still don’t always feel that spark. Or I can’t tell if I’m not interested because the interest isn’t there, or because I’m already anticipating that it’ll take effort and maybe not feel rewarding enough.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it and say, “No, never, I feel nothing,” because that sounds too absolute. But I also can’t remember a recent time where I woke up rested and suddenly felt this clear renewed enjoyment, like, “Oh yeah, I’m back, I want to do all these things again.” It’s more vague than that. Sometimes I can do something and it’s fine once I’m in it, but the wanting beforehand… that part still feels kind of muted, even when there isn’t a specific crisis or pressure that day.\n\nDoctor: So you mentioned that your mood is usually low most of the time — can you confirm if there have been any days at all in the past several weeks where you felt clearly better, even if just for a few hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, there are moments where I can seem a little better, I guess. Like if something at work goes smoothly, or someone is nice to me, or I’m distracted for a bit, I might not be actively thinking about how bad I feel. So in that sense, yes, there are a few hours here and there where it’s not as noticeable.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I feel clearly better in the way I think you mean. It’s more like the heaviness gets pushed to the background for a while. I came in because for a long time now I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling, like even when work is going okay or people are treating me well, I still feel down and kind of empty most days.\n\nSo if you ask whether there are brief windows where I can function or look normal, yes. But if you mean do I have days where I feel like myself again, or genuinely lighter, that’s much harder to say. Mostly it’s still there, just quieter sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there have been any stretches of time in the past few weeks — even just two or three days in a row — where you felt noticeably better, where the low mood seemed to ease up for a while, even if it came back afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I’ve been trying to think about that because I know a few hours here and there can blur together, but I can’t remember a solid stretch of two or three days where I thought, “Okay, this has actually lifted.”\n\nIt’s more that the low feeling is just kind of… the baseline. Like I came in because this has been going on for a long time, where I’m walking around with this flat, heavy feeling most days. Even when work is fine, even when nothing bad is happening, even when people are nice to me, I still feel down and kind of empty underneath it.\n\nThere might be parts of a day where I’m distracted or I’m functioning better, but I don’t think I’ve had a run of days recently where I felt noticeably better in a real way. If it eases up, it’s more like it gets quieter for a bit, not like it actually goes away.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy, and whether you still feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I have a really clear example, honestly. I can think of things I used to do more easily, like watching certain shows, playing a game, listening to music, stuff like that, but when I try to answer whether I still get pleasure from them now, it’s kind of hard to pin down.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll put something on because I remember that I used to enjoy it, or because it feels like the kind of thing I should do to relax, but I don’t know if I’m actually enjoying it in the same way. A lot of the time it’s more like I’m passing time. I might be occupied, and maybe there are moments where I think, “Okay, this is fine,” but it doesn’t really stay with me as satisfying afterward.\n\nI don’t want to say I get absolutely nothing from anything, because that seems too extreme. But if you’re asking about that real feeling of looking forward to it, getting into it, feeling better because I did it… that’s the part that feels pretty muted. Sometimes I can do the activity, but the pleasure part is not really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you did something you used to really enjoy, and during that moment, you genuinely felt pleasure or satisfaction from it?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and honestly I’m not sure I can point to a recent moment where I’d say, yes, that was genuine pleasure or satisfaction.\n\nLike, I’ve done things that used to be enjoyable. I’ve put on music, or watched something I used to like, or messed around with a game for a while. And in the moment I might not be miserable exactly — sometimes it’s more neutral, like I’m occupied and time is passing. But when I check in with myself, it doesn’t really feel like, “Oh, I’m glad I did that,” or “That felt good.” It’s more like I was trying to recreate a feeling I remember having, but it doesn’t quite show up.\n\nThere might be tiny moments where I smile at something or think something is kind of funny, but it feels very brief and thin, if that makes sense. I don’t really come away from it feeling satisfied. So… no, I can’t really think of a clear recent example where I did something I used to really enjoy and actually felt that same pleasure from it.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm whether your low mood has been consistently present without significant improvement, even for a few consecutive days, over the past several weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much how it’s been. I can’t really point to a few days in a row where I felt like it had lifted in any meaningful way.\n\nI mean, there are moments where I’m distracted or I’m doing what I need to do, so maybe from the outside it doesn’t look constant. So in that sense, yes, there are a few hours here and there where it’s not as noticeable.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I feel clearly better in the way I think you mean. It’s more like the heaviness gets pushed to the background for a while. I came in because for a long time now I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling, like even when work is going okay or people are treating me well, I still feel down and kind of empty most days.\n\nSo if you ask whether there are brief windows where I can function or look normal, yes. But if you mean do I have days where I feel like myself again, or genuinely lighter, that’s much harder to say. Mostly it’s still there, just quieter sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there have been any stretches of time in the past few weeks — even just two or three days in a row — where you felt noticeably better, where the low mood seemed to ease up for a while, even if it came back afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I’ve been trying to think about that because I know a few hours here and there can blur together, but I can’t remember a solid stretch of two or three days where I thought, “Okay, this has actually lifted.”\n\nIt’s more that the low feeling is just kind of… the baseline. Like I came in because this has been going on for a long time, where I’m walking around with this flat, heavy feeling most days. Even when work is fine, even when nothing bad is happening, even when people are nice to me, I still feel down and kind of empty underneath it.\n\nThere might be parts of a day where I’m distracted or I’m functioning better, but I don’t think I’ve had a run of days recently where I felt noticeably better in a real way. If it eases up, it’s more like it gets quieter for a bit, not like it actually goes away.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy, and whether you still feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I have a really clear example, honestly. I can think of things I used to do more easily, like watching certain shows, playing a game, listening to music, stuff like that, but when I try to answer whether I still get pleasure from them now, it’s kind of hard to pin down.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll put something on because I remember that I used to enjoy it, or because it feels like the kind of thing I should do to relax, but I don’t know if I’m actually enjoying it in the same way. A lot of the time it’s more like I’m passing time. I might be occupied, and maybe there are moments where I think, “Okay, this is fine,” but it doesn’t really stay with me as satisfying afterward.\n\nI don’t want to say I get absolutely nothing from anything, because that seems too extreme. But if you’re asking about that real feeling of looking forward to it, getting into it, feeling better because I did it… that’s the part that feels pretty muted. Sometimes I can do the activity, but the pleasure part is not really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you did something you used to really enjoy, and during that moment, you genuinely felt pleasure or satisfaction from it?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and honestly I’m not sure I can point to a recent moment where I’d say, yes, that was genuine pleasure or satisfaction.\n\nLike, I’ve done things that used to be enjoyable. I’ve put on music, or watched something I used to like, or messed around with a game for a while. And in the moment I might not be miserable exactly — sometimes it’s more neutral, like I’m occupied and time is passing. But when I check in with myself, it doesn’t really feel like, “Oh, I’m glad I did that,” or “That felt good.” It’s more like I was trying to recreate a feeling I remember having, but it doesn’t quite show up.\n\nThere might be tiny moments where I smile at something or think something is kind of funny, but it feels very brief and thin, if that makes sense. I don’t really come away from it feeling satisfied. So… no, I can’t really think of a clear recent example where I did something I used to really enjoy and actually felt that same pleasure from it.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm whether your low mood has been consistently present without significant improvement, even for a few consecutive days, over the past several weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much how it’s been. I can’t really point to a few days in a row where I felt like it had lifted in any meaningful way.\n\nI mean, there are moments where I’m distracted or I’m doing what I need to do, so maybe from the outside it doesn’t look constant. Like work can be going okay, or someone can be perfectly nice to me, and nothing especially bad is happening. But underneath that, I still have this flat, heavy feeling most days. It’s like I’m carrying it around with me, and even when the day is technically fine, I still feel down and kind of empty.\n\nSo yeah, over the past several weeks, I don’t think there’s been a real break from it for a few consecutive days. Maybe it gets a little quieter for parts of a day, but not enough that I’d call it significant improvement."
